========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… the main thing is I don’t feel like myself emotionally. Like, something in me just kind of shut off. I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.

And that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.

No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.

It’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.

I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.

Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.

But now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.

I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.

If I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.

It’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.0, F32.1, F32.2, F32.3, F32.8, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I don’t feel like myself emotionally. Like, something in me just kind of shut off. I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. And then afterward it was almost immediate, or maybe over the next day or two… I just noticed this emptiness settling in. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. I know in my head, “This is supposed to feel nice. This is my husband, this is our normal evening, this is something I usually enjoy.” But there’s just… nothing. No warmth, no little happy feeling. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. And then afterward it was almost immediate, or maybe over the next day or two… I just noticed this emptiness settling in. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. I know in my head, “This is supposed to feel nice. This is my husband, this is our normal evening, this is something I usually enjoy.” But there’s just… nothing. No warmth, no little happy feeling. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there.     "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.

I’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.

So I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.

I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.

Getting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.

So, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I don’t feel like myself emotionally. Like, something in me just kind of shut off. I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. And then afterward it was almost immediate, or maybe over the next day or two… I just noticed this emptiness settling in. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. I know in my head, “This is supposed to feel nice. This is my husband, this is our normal evening, this is something I usually enjoy.” But there’s just… nothing. No warmth, no little happy feeling. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. Not like wanting to die, no. I’ve been really upset and overwhelmed, and there are moments where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I want a break from feeling like this, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn out and kind of scared by how different I feel, if that makes sense. But I don’t have a plan or anything like that, and I don’t want to do something to myself. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. I’ve been really upset and overwhelmed, and there are moments where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I want a break from feeling like this, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn out and kind of scared by how different I feel, if that makes sense. But I don’t have a plan or anything like that, and I don’t want to do something to myself. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you noticed your clothes feeling looser on you since the accident?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 36 / stocker_or_order_filler / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I haven’t been paying super close attention to how my clothes fit, honestly. I’m mostly just putting on whatever’s clean and getting through the day.

Nothing has stood out like my jeans suddenly falling down or my shirts hanging differently. Maybe my appetite and routine have been a little off here and there just from stress, but as far as clothes feeling looser, I don’t think so. If it’s changed, it hasn’t been enough for me to notice.

I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure by a number or anything. We have a scale somewhere, but I honestly haven’t been checking it.

I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. And then afterward it was almost immediate, or maybe over the next day or two… I just noticed this emptiness settling in. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. I know in my head, “This is supposed to feel nice. This is my husband, this is our normal evening, this is something I usually enjoy.” But there’s just… nothing. No warmth, no little happy feeling. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. Not like wanting to die, no. I’ve been really upset and overwhelmed, and there are moments where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I want a break from feeling like this, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn out and kind of scared by how different I feel, if that makes sense. But I don’t have a plan or anything like that, and I don’t want to do something to myself. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes feeling looser on you since the accident?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I haven’t been paying super close attention to how my clothes fit, honestly. I’m mostly just putting on whatever’s clean and getting through the day.\n\nNothing has stood out like my jeans suddenly falling down or my shirts hanging differently. Maybe my appetite and routine have been a little off here and there just from stress, but as far as clothes feeling looser, I don’t think so. If it’s changed, it hasn’t been enough for me to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the day, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty down or just kind of heavy, but it’s not like I wake up and stay sad all day long.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, if that makes sense. I’ll be okay for a while, or at least distracted, and then something will hit me and my mood drops. But “sad most of the day” sounds stronger than what I’d say. I’m not happy, exactly, but I don’t know if sadness is the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure by a number or anything. We have a scale somewhere, but I honestly haven’t been checking it.\n\nFrom what I can tell, my clothes fit about the same, and nobody’s said anything to me like I look thinner. I know sometimes after something stressful people can drop weight without realizing it, so I guess it’s possible there’s been a little change, but nothing noticeable to me. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. I know in my head, “This is supposed to feel nice. This is my husband, this is our normal evening, this is something I usually enjoy.” But there’s just… nothing. No warmth, no little happy feeling. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. That sounds so specific, like crying all day or feeling hopeless in a way I can point to, and I don’t know if that’s what this is.\n\nIt’s more like… I feel blank. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. There are moments where I think, “I should be upset,” or “I should feel normal,” but it’s not a steady sadness all day, if that makes sense. I haven’t really been tracking it like that. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. Not like wanting to die, no. I’ve been really upset and overwhelmed, and there are moments where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I want a break from feeling like this, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn out and kind of scared by how different I feel, if that makes sense. But I don’t have a plan or anything like that, and I don’t want to do something to myself. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes feeling looser on you since the accident?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I haven’t been paying super close attention to how my clothes fit, honestly. I’m mostly just putting on whatever’s clean and getting through the day.\n\nNothing has stood out like my jeans suddenly falling down or my shirts hanging differently. Maybe my appetite and routine have been a little off here and there just from stress, but as far as clothes feeling looser, I don’t think so. If it’s changed, it hasn’t been enough for me to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the day, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty down or just kind of heavy, but it’s not like I wake up and stay sad all day long.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, if that makes sense. I’ll be okay for a while, or at least distracted, and then something will hit me and my mood drops. But “sad most of the day” sounds stronger than what I’d say. I’m not happy, exactly, but I don’t know if sadness is the main thing.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no. I mean, I haven’t weighed myself, so I can’t say for sure by a number or anything. We have a scale somewhere, but I honestly haven’t been checking it.\n\nFrom what I can tell, my clothes fit about the same, and nobody’s said anything to me like I look thinner. I know sometimes after something stressful people can drop weight without realizing it, so I guess it’s possible there’s been a little change, but nothing noticeable to me. If I had lost a lot, I think I’d have noticed my pants fitting different or my work clothes feeling baggy, and that hasn’t really happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had people comment on your weight loss since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Nobody’s said anything like, “You look like you’ve lost weight,” or asked if I’m eating enough, or anything like that.\n\nI feel like if it were noticeable, someone probably would’ve said something—my family especially tends to comment on stuff like that, even when you don’t ask them to. But I haven’t gotten any comments. I also haven’t really been watching for it myself, so I guess I can’t say nothing has changed at all, but as far as people pointing it out or me being told I look thinner, no.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless.” That word feels… I don’t know, really big and kind of harsh. I haven’t been walking around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly.

A burden, though… I guess I worry sometimes that people are tired of me not being myself, or having to check on me, or deal with me being kind of off. But I don’t know if that’s the same as truly feeling like I’m a burden. It’s more like I feel guilty if I need extra patience, if that makes sense.

So I wouldn’t say it’s this constant belief that I’m worthless or that everyone would be better off without me. It’s more like I get uncomfortable needing help or attention, and then I start overthinking whether I’m making things harder for other people.

I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes feeling looser on you since the accident?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I haven’t been paying super close attention to how my clothes fit, honestly. I’m mostly just putting on whatever’s clean and getting through the day.\n\nNothing has stood out like my jeans suddenly falling down or my shirts hanging differently. Maybe my appetite and routine have been a little off here and there just from stress, but as far as clothes feeling looser, I don’t think so. If it’s changed, it hasn’t been enough for me to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the day, no. We have a scale somewhere, but I honestly haven’t been checking it.\n\nFrom what I can tell, my clothes fit about the same, and nobody’s said anything to me like I look thinner. I know sometimes after something stressful people can drop weight without realizing it, so I guess it’s possible there’s been a little change, but nothing noticeable to me. If I had lost a lot, I think I’d have noticed my pants fitting different or my work clothes feeling baggy, and that hasn’t really happened.\n\nDoctor: Have you had people comment on your weight loss since the accident?\n\nPatient: No, not really. Nobody’s said anything like, “You look like you’ve lost weight,” or asked if I’m eating enough, or anything like that.\n\nI feel like if it were noticeable, someone probably would’ve said something—my family especially tends to comment on stuff like that, even when you don’t ask them to. But I haven’t gotten any comments. I also haven’t really been watching for it myself, so I guess I can’t say nothing has changed at all, but as far as people pointing it out or me being told I look thinner, no.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling worthless or like you're a burden to others since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d put it as “worthless.” That word feels… I don’t know, really big and kind of harsh. I haven’t been walking around thinking, “I’m worthless,” exactly.\n\nA burden, though… I guess I worry sometimes that people are tired of me not being myself, or having to check on me, or deal with me being kind of off. But I don’t know if that’s the same as truly feeling like I’m a burden. It’s more like I feel guilty if I need extra patience, if that makes sense.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say it’s this constant belief that I’m worthless or that everyone would be better off without me. I’m not totally sure. I haven’t really been tracking it, like I’m not counting meals or noticing portions in a clear way. I think my appetite is probably… mostly the same? Maybe some days I’m not as interested in food, but I can’t tell if that’s a real change or just normal day-to-day stuff.

I’m still eating, and it’s not like I’ve suddenly stopped wanting food altogether or anything dramatic like that. I guess if you asked me to compare before and after exactly, I’d have a hard time answering. Nothing has stood out to me as a big appetite change, at least not enough that I’d immediately say, “Yes, that’s different since the accident.”

It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me. So, yeah—roughly three weeks since that happened.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to enjoy activities or hobbies that you used to find pleasurable?\n\nPatient: No… not really. That’s actually the part that feels the most wrong to me. I’ll try to do the things I normally do after work, like sitting with my husband or putting on one of my shows, because usually that would help me unwind. Even if I was tired, I’d still feel some kind of comfort from it, or I’d laugh a little, or just feel glad to be home.\n\nBut now it’s like I’m going through the motions. It’s like that part of me shut off, and I keep waiting for it to come back, but it doesn’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty about anything related to the accident at work?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it guilt exactly. I’ve gone over it in my head a lot, like anyone would, I guess—what I was doing, where I was standing, whether I should have noticed something sooner—but I can’t really say there’s one clear thing I feel guilty about.\n\nIt’s more like… I keep circling around the whole situation, trying to make sense of it, but I don’t have a specific “this was my fault” feeling. At least not that I can put into words. I just know it happened, and it was awful, and afterward I haven’t felt right.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or depressed most of the day since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad or depressed, exactly. Heavy sometimes, maybe, but mostly just kind of hollow and not like myself. I just know since the accident I haven’t felt right emotionally, and I keep waiting to feel something normal again.\n\nDoctor: Are you still able to find pleasure in any activities or things that you used to enjoy before the accident?\n\nPatient: Not really, no. I mean, I keep trying, because I don’t want to just sit there and stare at the wall, and part of me thinks if I do the normal things, maybe I’ll feel normal for a minute. Like I’ll sit with my husband after work, or put on one of my shows—the kind of stuff that used to make me feel settled, like, okay, I’m home, the day is over.\n\nBut now I just… know I’m supposed to enjoy it. I can recognize it in my head, like, this should feel comforting, or this scene should make me laugh, or being next to him should make me feel warm and safe. And instead there’s just nothing there. It’s like the warm part of me shut off. That’s honestly one of the reasons I came in, because it feels so unlike me. I’m doing the same things I used to enjoy, but I can’t really feel the pleasure from them.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious since the accident at work?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I mean… I don’t always think of it as “anxious” in the way people say it, like butterflies or worrying about regular stuff. It’s more like my whole body is on alert all the time when I’m anywhere near receiving now.\n\nIf I go back there, even just close to that area, I’m checking everything without meaning to. I’m looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching where the forklifts or people are moving, looking for where the exits are. And if someone comes up behind me or moves too fast, I’m already turning to see what they’re doing before I even realize I’m doing it.\n\nIt’s like I can’t just be on the floor and trust that it’s safe anymore. I keep thinking I have to notice every little thing, because if I miss something for even a second, something bad could happen again. So yes, I guess that is anxiety. It just feels more like I can’t relax or let my guard down, especially near that part of work.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight since the accident?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself regularly, so I can’t say for sure down to a number or anything. My clothes don’t feel dramatically different, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nI guess I’ve been paying more attention to how I feel than to the scale, if that makes sense. If I did lose a couple pounds or something, I wouldn’t really know unless I checked. But no, I haven’t noticed a significant weight loss.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any thoughts of not wanting to live or hurting yourself since the accident?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. Not like wanting to die, no. I’ve been really upset and overwhelmed, and there are moments where I just want everything to stop for a while, like I want a break from feeling like this, but I haven’t been thinking about hurting myself.\n\nIt’s more that I feel worn out and kind of scared by how different I feel, if that makes sense. But I don’t have a plan or anything like that, and I don’t want to do something to myself. I just want to feel normal again.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in how active or restless you are since the accident?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. Not like I’m pacing around all day for no reason, exactly, but when I’m anywhere near receiving, I get… keyed up. Like my body starts doing this checking thing before I even decide to do it.\n\nI’ll be looking up at the racks, checking how high the pallets are stacked, watching who’s moving around me, where the forklifts are, where the exits are. If someone walks behind me or I hear movement, I turn fast, like I need to know exactly where they are. It’s hard to just stand there and do my job normally because I feel like I have to keep scanning everything.\n\nSo I guess I’m more restless in that way. I can’t just be still and trust the floor is safe anymore, not for even a minute.\n\nDoctor: Have you felt like you're moving slower than usual or having trouble getting started on things since the accident?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say I’m moving slower, exactly. Like physically, I can still get up and walk around and do things. I don’t think people would look at me and say I’m dragging or anything like that.\n\nGetting started on things… maybe a little, but I don’t know if it’s in the way you mean. Sometimes I sort of sit there longer than I normally would before doing regular stuff, like getting ready or starting chores, because I feel kind of stuck in my head. But once I’m actually doing the thing, I can usually keep going.\n\nSo, no, I haven’t noticed a big slow-down, not like my body won’t move. It’s more that I hesitate sometimes, and I have to talk myself into starting, but I don’t know if that counts.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed your clothes feeling looser on you since the accident?\n\nPatient: Um… I haven’t really noticed that, no. I mean, I haven’t been paying super close attention to how my clothes fit, honestly. I’m mostly just putting on whatever’s clean and getting through the day.\n\nNothing has stood out like my jeans suddenly falling down or my shirts hanging differently. Maybe my appetite and routine have been a little off here and there just from stress, but as far as clothes feeling looser, I don’t think so. If it’s changed, it hasn’t been enough for me to notice.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel sad most of the day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I’d say sad most of the day, no. I mean, I have moments where I feel pretty down or just kind of heavy, but it’s not like I wake up and stay sad all day long.\n\nIt’s more like it comes in waves, if that makes sense. I’ll be okay for a while, or at least distracted, and then something will hit me and my mood drops. But “sad most of the day” sounds stronger than what I’d say. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I… I don’t really know how to answer that cleanly.

I don’t think I feel guilty in the sense of, like, “This was my fault” or “I caused what happened.” I haven’t been able to sort it into something that simple. But when you say my coworker, it does make me feel kind of tight in my chest, because I think about them and I think about how unfair it was, and then I start going over little pieces of it in my head, like whether there was anything I should’ve noticed or done differently.

But I don’t know if that’s guilt, exactly. It’s more like… I feel awful that it happened to them, and I hate that they were involved at all. Sometimes I catch myself thinking, “Why them too?” or “Could I have done something?” But I also don’t have a clear answer to that.

So, I guess I’d say I feel really bad about it, and I get stuck thinking about it, but I’m not sure I’d call it guilt exactly.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: I guess… the main thing is I don’t feel like myself emotionally. Like, something in me just kind of shut off. I’ll be sitting with my husband after work, or trying to watch one of my shows—something that normally would feel comforting, even just a little relaxing or happy—and I know in my head that I should feel that, but I don’t. It’s just blank.\n\nAnd that’s really what scared me enough to come in, because I’m not usually like this. I keep waiting for that warm feeling to come back, like when you’re with someone you love or doing something familiar, but it’s like there’s nothing there. Just… empty, I guess.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this emotional emptiness?\n\nPatient: About three weeks now. It started after the accident at work, when that pallet collapsed and my coworker got hurt. I keep thinking, like, before that day I was tired or stressed sometimes, sure, but I still felt like me.
